## Reasoning

### Understanding the Mission
- Score is MINIMISED (lower = better, negative = good)
- Robots should collect items from ONE half of the source (either BLUE or GREEN side) and bring them to the nest
- Items from one half decrease score by 1 (good), items from the other half increase score by 1 (bad)
- Need robots to: navigate to source (BLACK floor), pick up items, return to nest (WHITE floor)

### Analysis of Champion (-0.28)
The champion uses:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(cyan=6) → goes away from cyan walls, transitions on many neighbors
- State 1: GoToColor(yellow=4) → moves toward yellow, multiple transitions including on BLACK floor
- State 2: Attraction with transitions on WHITE floor and GRAY floor
- State 3: Attraction with transitions on many neighbors or fixed probability

The champion seems to be doing some foraging. The key insight: robots should go to BLACK floor (source), then return to WHITE floor (nest). The BLUE wall is color 3, GREEN wall is color 2. We want to consistently visit ONE side.

### Strategy
- **State 0**: Explore/go toward source (BLACK floor)
- **State 1**: At source (BLACK), go toward one specific color wall (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) to get consistent item type
- **State 2**: Return to nest (go toward RED wall=1, or away from colored walls, or go to WHITE floor)
- **State 3**: At nest (WHITE), deliver and restart

1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Small edits to champion (5 machines)

## Reasoning

### Key Insight from Round 2
The champion (-2.29) is a 3-state machine:
- **State 0**: GoToColor(GREEN=2) → moves toward green walls (one half of source). Transitions: on BLACK floor (p=0.9) → state 1, on WHITE floor (p=0.7) → state 1, sees GREEN (p=0.75) → state 1
- **State 1**: GoToColor(RED=1) → moves toward red wall (nest). Transitions: on WHITE floor (p=0.9) → state 0, on GRAY floor (p=0.15) → state 1 (self-loop avoided, goes to state 1... wait, destination 1 from state 1 would be state... let me re-read)

Wait - with 3 states, from state 1 the "others" are [0, 2]. So destination 1 = state 2.
- State 1 transitions: on WHITE (p=0.9) → state 0, on GRAY (p=0.15) → state 2, see RED (p=0.8) → state 2
- **State 2**: Exploration (rwm=30). Transitions: on BLACK (p=0.8) → state 0, on WHITE (p=0.7) → state 1

So the flow is: explore → go to GREEN source → go toward RED nest → deliver → repeat. The GREEN side decreases score (good!). BLUE side increases score (bad). This confirms GREEN=2 is the good source.

### Strategy
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Tune parameters of the champion (probabilities, transition conditions)
3. **Exploration**: Try 4-state versions, different exploration behaviors, stronger transitions

Key findings to avoid: using clr=3 (BLUE) leads to positive scores. GREEN (clr=2) is the good side.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

### Champion Analysis (-5.90)
The champion is a 2-state machine:
- **State 0**: GoToColor(GREEN=2) → go toward green walls (source). Transitions: on BLACK (p=0.9)→state 0 (self!? wait...), on WHITE (p=0.7)→state 0, see GREEN (p=0.75)→state 0

Wait - with 2 states, from state 0 the only other state is state 1. So `--n0x0 0` means destination index 0 = state 1 (the first "other" state). All three transitions from state 0 go to state 1!

So:
- **State 0**: GoToColor(GREEN=2). Transitions: BLACK→state1, WHITE→state1, see GREEN→state1
- **State 1**: GoToColor(RED=1). Transitions: WHITE→state0, GRAY→state0, see RED→state0

This is elegant: go to green source, pick up item, then go to red nest wall to deliver. The 2-state simplicity is powerful.

### Strategy
The key insight: BLACK floor = at source (got item), WHITE floor = at nest (deliver). The champion uses floor color to switch between "go to source" and "go to nest". 

For exploitation: tune probabilities, try different thresholds, add transitions.
For exploration: try adding a 3rd state for exploration/recovery, or use GoAwayColor for the return trip.

Note: the 3-state versions scored around -2.5, suggesting the 2-state version is already very effective. Focus exploitation heavily on the champion's structure.

Key: `--n0x0 0` with 2 states means destination 0 = the only other state. This is correct.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

### Champion Analysis (-9.67)
The key insight from Round 5: using **GoAwayColor(RED=1)** for state 1 (behavior B=9) instead of GoToColor(RED=1) (B=8) gave the best result! This makes sense - GoAwayColor from RED means moving away from the red wall, which pushes robots toward the source side. Wait, actually GoAwayColor(RED) would move away from the red nest wall... but the champion uses it for the "return to nest" state. 

Actually re-reading: State 1 is entered when the robot is on BLACK (source) or WHITE (nest) or sees GREEN. Then state 1 does GoAwayColor(RED=1) which moves AWAY from the red wall. But the red wall is at the nest side... so going away from red = going toward source? That seems wrong.

Wait - maybe I have the arena orientation wrong. The RED wall is on the NEST side. GoAwayColor(RED) = move away from red = move toward source. But that's state 1 which should be "return to nest"...

Actually, looking at the flow: state 0 = GoToColor(GREEN) gets items from green source. Transitions to state 1 when on BLACK, WHITE, or see GREEN. State 1 = GoAwayColor(RED) = move away from red wall = move toward source area. But transitions back to state 0 when on WHITE, GRAY, or see RED...

Hmm, perhaps the robots oscillate effectively: state 0 goes toward green (source), transitions to state 1 on BLACK (at source, has item), state 1 goes away from red (toward source again? No - away from red = toward the opposite wall = toward source). Then sees RED → back to state 0.

Actually wait - GoAwayColor moves away from the nearest visible colored object. If the robot is near the source (black floor, far from red wall), it might not see red well, and GoAwayColor(RED) would just explore. When it gets close to the nest (red wall visible), it transitions back.

The key lesson: **B=9 (GoAwayColor) for state 1 works better than B=8 (GoToColor)**. Let me focus exploitation on this.

### Exploitation Strategy
- Tune probabilities around champion values (±0.05)
- Try GoAwayColor(RED) with slight parameter variations
- Try adding 4th transition to state 1
- Try mixing GoToColor(GREEN) with GoAwayColor(GREEN) for state 0
- The GRAY transition probability 0.2 seems important - try varying it

## Reasoning

### Champion Analysis (-9.88)
The champion uses:
- **State 0**: GoToColor(GREEN=2). Transitions: BLACK(0.85)→s1, WHITE(0.8)→s1, seeGREEN(0.7)→s1
- **State 1**: GoAwayColor(RED=1). Transitions: WHITE(0.82)→s0, GRAY(0.22)→s0, seeRED(0.72)→s0

The GoAwayColor(RED) in state 1 is key - when a robot has an item and is heading back, it moves away from the red wall. But wait - moving away from red = moving toward source. This seems counterintuitive but it's scoring -9.88 which is excellent.

Actually, GoAwayColor moves away from the nearest detected red object. If the red wall is behind the robot (nest side), moving away from it means moving toward the source. But that would make robots go back to source again... Unless the robot is already at the nest/white area when it enters state 1, and the transition WHITE(0.82) quickly sends it back to state 0.

The key parameter difference from the previous champion: state 1 transitions have slightly lower probabilities (0.82, 0.22, 0.72 vs 0.85, 0.2, 0.75). This suggests the robot spends slightly more time in state 1, which might help navigation.

### Fine-tuning Strategy
Focus on:
1. Exact copy of champion (elitism)
2. Small variations of state 1 transition probabilities around (0.82, 0.22, 0.72)
3. Small variations of state 0 transition probabilities around (0.85, 0.8, 0.7)
4. Try adding 4th transition to state 0
5. Try variations with state 1 having 4 transitions
6. Avoid: adding GRAY transition to state 0 (not in champion), avoid high probability for WHITE return (0.9+)

The convergence is strong - keep exploiting this 2-state GoToColor/GoAwayColor structure with GREEN source and RED nest.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 24 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.64 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
